[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Combined Presentation), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\n(this is the start of the consultation)\n\n[Last Question]\nHello, I’m Dr. ____. What brings you in today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 66 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Feeling_Restless\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I feel like I cannot settle inside my own skin anymore, like I have to jump up and do the next thing even when I am worn out and should be sitting still.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hello, Doctor. It's nice to meet you. Well, I suppose I'm here because... well, I've been meaning to make an appointment for a while now, but I just kept putting it off. But it’s gotten to the point where I just feel like I cannot settle inside my own skin anymore. It’s the most awful, frustrating feeling. 

My husband bought me this lovely plush recliner a while back when I stopped working, so I could finally just rest, you know? But I can't even use it. I'll sit down with a magazine or something, and I swear, not even two minutes go by and I just get this... this crawling feeling, like I absolutely have to jump up and do the next thing. And the silliest part of it all is that I am completely worn out. I'm 66 years old, I'm exhausted, and I really should be sitting still and just enjoying a cup of tea or a television program. But I just can't do it. I'm up reorganizing the linen closet for the third time this month or pacing around the kitchen wiping down counters that are already clean, and I just don't know how to turn it off. It's like my body is forcing me to keep moving even though I'm so tired I could cry.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, it’s nothing like that. I mean, I don't sit there thinking the house is going to burn down or that someone is going to get hurt if I don't fold the towels right that second. It’s not a worry in my head, if that makes sense. It's more like... well, it’s just this physical urge. Like an itch you can't reach, but it's on the inside. 

I remember last Tuesday, my husband, Jim—he's such a patient man, bless him—he had made us a nice pot of Earl Grey, and he said, "Martha, just sit with me for a bit. The news is on." And I really wanted to. I had my cup, I had a little biscuit, and I sat in that recliner. But almost immediately, my legs started to feel twitchy. Not painful, just... restless. And then my mind just started zooming to the fact that the dog's toys were scattered in the hallway, and the mail from yesterday was still on the entryway table, and before I knew it, I had left my tea on the coaster—which I had to straighten three times, mind you—and I was up gathering the toys. Jim just sighed. He didn't say anything, but I could tell he was disappointed. And I was disappointed in myself! I just wanted to drink my tea. But there’s no voice telling me something terrible will happen, it’s just this motor inside me that won't shut off, even when my bones are aching and I just want to rest.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I wouldn't say I'm a worrier. I mean, everyone worries a little bit, don't they? Like when Jim's cholesterol was high last year, of course I worried about that, and I made sure we switched to leaner meats and cut back on the butter, which was hard because Jim loves his butter on a baked potato. But no, I don't just sit around agonizing over things for no reason. It's not my mind that's troubled like that, it's just my body that won't stay put. 

My sister, Elaine, now she's a worrier. She'll call me up on a Tuesday morning and talk for an hour about how she's sure the neighborhood stray cat is going to freeze, or how she thinks her grandson isn't eating enough vegetables, and she just works herself up into a real state over it. I always tell her, "Elaine, you can't control the whole world," but she just can't help it. I'm not like that at all. My mind is busy, yes, but it's busy thinking about the fifty things I need to do around the house, not worrying that the sky is falling. Like yesterday, I wasn't worried about the laundry, I just couldn't stop thinking about the fact that the towels in the guest bathroom needed to be washed, dried, folded, and put back before I could even think about sitting down. And then once that was done, I noticed the baseboards... but it's never out of fear or anxiety, just this endless to-do list that my brain won't let me ignore.

Medications? Oh, goodness, let me think. No, I haven't changed a single thing. I mean, I take a little calcium for my bones, you know, just what Dr. Henderson—my regular doctor—told me to take years ago. He always says, "Martha, at your age, we just need to keep the bones strong," so I take that, and a standard daily multivitamin, but I've been taking those exact same ones since I turned sixty. I buy the big bottles at Costco so I don't have to keep going back every month. 

Jim, now, he has a whole plastic pillbox because of the cholesterol I mentioned, and his blood pressure, and I'm the one who organizes all of that for him every Sunday evening right after dinner. I have this little system with the days of the week, because otherwise he'd just forget entirely and leave them sitting right there on the kitchen island. But for me? No, nothing new. 

My sister Elaine is always trying to get me to take these herbal things she reads about on the internet—she sent me a whole care package of some root powder last fall that she swore would give me more energy, which is just a laugh because energy is the absolute last thing I need right now! I told her, "Elaine, I'm practically vibrating out of my chair most days, why on earth would I take an energy pill?" So I just put that whole bottle straight in the trash. I don't like messing with things like that anyway, you never know how it's going to react with your stomach or if it's even safe. So, to answer your question, no, no new medications or supplements or anything at all. It's just my same old routine, which is why it's so baffling to me that I can't just sit still. It's not coming from a pill, it's just... me.

I always tell her, \"Elaine, you can't control the whole world,\" but she just can't help it. I'm not like that at all. My mind is busy, yes, but it's busy thinking about the fifty things I need to do around the house, not worrying that the sky is falling. Like yesterday, I wasn't worried about the laundry, I just couldn't stop thinking about the fact that the towels in the guest bathroom needed to be washed, dried, folded, and put back before I could even think about sitting down. And then once that was done, I noticed the baseboards... but it's never out of fear or anxiety, just this endless to-do list that my brain won't let me ignore.\n\nDoctor: Have you started or changed any medications, supplements, or other substances around the time these symptoms began?\n\nPatient: Medications? Oh, goodness, let me think. No, I haven't changed a single thing. I mean, I take a little calcium for my bones, you know, just what Dr. Henderson—my regular doctor—told me to take years ago. He always says, \"Martha, at your age, we just need to keep the bones strong,\" so I take that, and a standard daily multivitamin, but I've been taking those exact same ones since I turned sixty. I buy the big bottles at Costco so I don't have to keep going back every month. \n\nJim, now, he has a whole plastic pillbox because of the cholesterol I mentioned, and his blood pressure, and I'm the one who organizes all of that for him every Sunday evening right after dinner. I have this little system with the days of the week, because otherwise he'd just forget entirely and leave them sitting right there on the kitchen island. But for me? No, nothing new. \n\nMy sister Elaine is always trying to get me to take these herbal things she reads about on the internet—she sent me a whole care package of some root powder last fall that she swore would give me more energy, which is just a laugh because energy is the absolute last thing I need right now! I told her, \"Elaine, I'm practically vibrating out of my chair most days, why on earth would I take an energy pill?\" So I just put that whole bottle straight in the trash. I don't like messing with things like that anyway, you never know how it's going to react with your stomach or if it's even safe. So, to answer your question, no, no new medications or supplements or anything at all. It's just my same old routine, which is why it's so baffling to me that I can't just sit still. It's not coming from a pill, it's just... me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No, no, nothing like that at all. Intrusive thoughts... no, I don't get anything like that. It's not like I'm seeing pictures in my head of terrible things happening, or having these awful thoughts that I just can't shake. It's really just the opposite, honestly. My mind is just so... practical, I suppose. 

Like I was saying about the towels, I just see that they need to be done, and then I have to do them. It's the same with the kitchen counters. After Jim makes his sandwich—he loves a turkey and Swiss on rye for lunch, every single day at noon—he always leaves a few crumbs on the counter. He tries to wipe them up, he really does, but he uses a dry paper towel and just sort of pushes them around. So I see the crumbs, and I think, "Well, I need to wipe those up properly." And then I get the sponge, and I wipe the crumbs, but then I see a little spot near the toaster, so I clean that, and then the toaster looks a bit smudged, so I get the stainless steel cleaner from under the sink... and before I know it, I've spent forty-five minutes scrubbing the entire kitchen, pulling the appliances out, wiping down the cabinet doors. 

But it's never because I had a bad thought! I'm not thinking, "Oh, if I don't clean this crumb, the whole house will be infested with bugs and we'll have to move." I don't think like that. It's just that I start a task, and then I see the next thing, and the next thing, and my body just won't let me stop until I'm absolutely exhausted. I just can't seem to put the brakes on, you know? But no, my mind isn't playing tricks on me with scary pictures or unwanted thoughts. It's just... I see a mess, and I have to fix it, right then and there.

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different things most days, even when there isn’t a specific reason to worry?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm a worrier. I mean, everyone worries a little bit, don't they? Like when Jim's cholesterol was high last year, of course I worried about that, and I made sure we switched to leaner meats and cut back on the butter, which was hard because Jim loves his butter on a baked potato. But no, I don't just sit around agonizing over things for no reason. It's not my mind that's troubled like that, it's just my body that won't stay put. \n\nMy sister, Elaine, now she's a worrier. She'll call me up on a Tuesday morning and talk for an hour about how she's sure the neighborhood stray cat is going to freeze, or how she thinks her grandson isn't eating enough vegetables, and she just works herself up into a real state over it. I always tell her, \"Elaine, you can't control the whole world,\" but she just can't help it. I'm not like that at all. My mind is busy, yes, but it's busy thinking about the fifty things I need to do around the house, not worrying that the sky is falling. Like yesterday, I wasn't worried about the laundry, I just couldn't stop thinking about the fact that the towels in the guest bathroom needed to be washed, dried, folded, and put back before I could even think about sitting down. And then once that was done, I noticed the baseboards... but it's never out of fear or anxiety, just this endless to-do list that my brain won't let me ignore.\n\nDoctor: Have you started or changed any medications, supplements, or other substances around the time these symptoms began?\n\nPatient: Medications? Oh, goodness, let me think. No, I haven't changed a single thing. I mean, I take a little calcium for my bones, you know, just what Dr. Henderson—my regular doctor—told me to take years ago. He always says, \"Martha, at your age, we just need to keep the bones strong,\" so I take that, and a standard daily multivitamin, but I've been taking those exact same ones since I turned sixty. I buy the big bottles at Costco so I don't have to keep going back every month. \n\nJim, now, he has a whole plastic pillbox because of the cholesterol I mentioned, and his blood pressure, and I'm the one who organizes all of that for him every Sunday evening right after dinner. I have this little system with the days of the week, because otherwise he'd just forget entirely and leave them sitting right there on the kitchen island. But for me? No, nothing new. \n\nMy sister Elaine is always trying to get me to take these herbal things she reads about on the internet—she sent me a whole care package of some root powder last fall that she swore would give me more energy, which is just a laugh because energy is the absolute last thing I need right now! I told her, \"Elaine, I'm practically vibrating out of my chair most days, why on earth would I take an energy pill?\" So I just put that whole bottle straight in the trash. I don't like messing with things like that anyway, you never know how it's going to react with your stomach or if it's even safe. So, to answer your question, no, no new medications or supplements or anything at all. It's just my same old routine, which is why it's so baffling to me that I can't just sit still. It's not coming from a pill, it's just... me.\n\nDoctor: When you feel driven to clean, straighten, or reorganize things, do you also get unwanted intrusive thoughts or images that keep coming back and make you feel you have to do it?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that at all. Intrusive thoughts... no, I don't get anything like that. It's not like I'm seeing pictures in my head of terrible things happening, or having these awful thoughts that I just can't shake. It's really just the opposite, honestly. My mind is just so... practical, I suppose. \n\nLike I was saying about the towels, I just see that they need to be done, and then I have to do them. It's the same with the kitchen counters. After Jim makes his sandwich—he loves a turkey and Swiss on rye for lunch, every single day at noon—he always leaves a few crumbs on the counter. He tries to wipe them up, he really does, but he uses a dry paper towel and just sort of pushes them around. So I see the crumbs, and I think, \"Well, I need to wipe those up properly.\" And then I get the sponge, and I wipe the crumbs, but then I see a little spot near the toaster, so I clean that, and then the toaster looks a bit smudged, so I get the stainless steel cleaner from under the sink... and before I know it, I've spent forty-five minutes scrubbing the entire kitchen, pulling the appliances out, wiping down the cabinet doors. \n\nBut it's never because I had a bad thought! I'm not thinking, \"Oh, if I don't clean this crumb, the whole house will be infested with bugs and we'll have to move.\" I don't think like that. It's just that I start a task, and then I see the next thing, and the next thing, and my body just won't let me stop until I'm absolutely exhausted. I just can't seem to put the brakes on, you know? But no, my mind isn't playing tricks on me with scary pictures or unwanted thoughts. It's just... I see a mess, and I have to fix it, right then and there.\n\n[Last Question]\nAt what age did these restlessness and compulsive cleaning symptoms first begin?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 66 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"Even back in grade school, my teachers wrote that I was bright but careless, always losing papers, talking out of turn, and needing reminders to stay in my seat or finish my work.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, goodness, what age? Well, if I really sit down and think about it—and Jim and I have actually talked about this, because he says I've been an absolute whirlwind since the day we met at the church social back in '76—it's not like I just woke up one day recently and started running around with a sponge. The truth is, it’s always been there. It's just changed shape, I suppose.

I mean, even way back in grade school... I remember my mother kept all my old report cards in this heavy cedar chest at the foot of her bed, and if you looked at them, every single teacher wrote the exact same thing. They'd say that I was a bright girl, but just so careless. I was always losing my spelling papers, or leaving my arithmetic homework sitting right on the kitchen table after my mother had sat there and made sure I did it. And the talking! Oh, I was always talking out of turn, just blurting things out before the teacher even finished her sentence. 

My third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—she was a lovely woman but very strict, wore these thick horn-rimmed glasses and always smelled like peppermint—she was constantly having to remind me to just stay in my seat. I just couldn't stay put, even then. I'd be up sharpening my pencil for the fifth time, or looking out the window, or trying to talk to the boy next to me. And finishing my work? Well, she was always having to stand over me to make sure I actually finished a worksheet, because I'd get halfway through and then I was off on something else entirely. 

So, you see, the restlessness isn't new at all. It’s just that back then, I was supposed to be sitting quietly at a little wooden desk, and now I have a whole house to manage. The cleaning is just what's in front of me to do now, but that feeling of a motor just running and running inside me, making me get up and move and bounce from one task to the next... that's been there since I was a little girl in pigtails. I just never really stopped to think about it as a problem until lately, because I'm getting older and I just wear myself out so completely.

So I see the crumbs, and I think, \"Well, I need to wipe those up properly.\" And then I get the sponge, and I wipe the crumbs, but then I see a little spot near the toaster, so I clean that, and then the toaster looks a bit smudged, so I get the stainless steel cleaner from under the sink... and before I know it, I've spent forty-five minutes scrubbing the entire kitchen, pulling the appliances out, wiping down the cabinet doors. \n\nBut it's never because I had a bad thought! I'm not thinking, \"Oh, if I don't clean this crumb, the whole house will be infested with bugs and we'll have to move.\" I don't think like that. It's just that I start a task, and then I see the next thing, and the next thing, and my body just won't let me stop until I'm absolutely exhausted. I just can't seem to put the brakes on, you know? But no, my mind isn't playing tricks on me with scary pictures or unwanted thoughts. It's just... I see a mess, and I have to fix it, right then and there.\n\nDoctor: At what age did these restlessness and compulsive cleaning symptoms first begin?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, what age? Well, if I really sit down and think about it—and Jim and I have actually talked about this, because he says I've been an absolute whirlwind since the day we met at the church social back in '76—it's not like I just woke up one day recently and started running around with a sponge. The truth is, it’s always been there. It's just changed shape, I suppose.\n\nI mean, even way back in grade school... I remember my mother kept all my old report cards in this heavy cedar chest at the foot of her bed, and if you looked at them, every single teacher wrote the exact same thing. They'd say that I was a bright girl, but just so careless. I was always losing my spelling papers, or leaving my arithmetic homework sitting right on the kitchen table after my mother had sat there and made sure I did it. And the talking! Oh, I was always talking out of turn, just blurting things out before the teacher even finished her sentence. \n\nMy third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—she was a lovely woman but very strict, wore these thick horn-rimmed glasses and always smelled like peppermint—she was constantly having to remind me to just stay in my seat. I just couldn't stay put, even then. I'd be up sharpening my pencil for the fifth time, or looking out the window, or trying to talk to the boy next to me. And finishing my work? Well, she was always having to stand over me to make sure I actually finished a worksheet, because I'd get halfway through and then I was off on something else entirely. \n\nSo, you see, the restlessness isn't new at all. It’s just that back then, I was supposed to be sitting quietly at a little wooden desk, and now I have a whole house to manage. The cleaning is just what's in front of me to do now, but that feeling of a motor just running and running inside me, making me get up and move and bounce from one task to the next... that's been there since I was a little girl in pigtails. I just never really stopped to think about it as a problem until lately, because I'm getting older and I just wear myself out so completely.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, goodness, sitting still... well, Jim actually gave up trying to take me to the picture show years ago. We used to go to the matinees over at the Bijou on Tuesdays because they have that nice senior discount, but I’d be up and down to the concession stand, or the restroom, or just wiggling in those velvet seats until poor Jim couldn't even hear the dialogue. So we just watch television at home now, where I can at least fold laundry or dust the coffee table while the screen is on. 

But you mentioned church, and oh, that is a whole other ordeal. We go to First Methodist, have been for forty years, and I am very involved with the Women's Auxiliary. We have our planning meetings every Wednesday morning in the fellowship hall. And even there, where I'm supposed to be paying attention to Pastor Dave or listening to the treasurer's report, I just cannot for the life of me sit quietly. It's honestly embarrassing. 

I'll be sitting there in those metal folding chairs—which are terribly uncomfortable to begin with, but I suppose that's beside the point—and I'll realize I'm just tapping my foot, tap-tap-tapping away on the linoleum. And if I force my foot to stop, then my hands start going. I carry this nice leather handbag, and I'll find myself just rubbing and rubbing the edge of my purse, over and over, just tracing the seam with my thumb. And then my back gets restless, so I'm shifting around in the chair, crossing my legs, uncrossing them, leaning this way and that, trying to get comfortable but never actually settling. 

It gets to the point where the ladies beside me—usually it's Susan Miller and Betty Higgins—they'll actually stop paying attention to the meeting and start looking over at me. Susan will give me this little sideways glance over her reading glasses, like she's wondering if I've had three pots of coffee for breakfast. I always try to just smile and pretend I'm just adjusting my skirt, but the truth is, I just feel like I'm going to jump right out of my skin if I have to sit frozen in one spot. It takes so much effort just to try and look like a normal person sitting in a chair, you know?

    "role": "system",

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F42.2, F41.1, F45.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m Dr. ____. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. It's nice to meet you. Well, I suppose I'm here because... well, I've been meaning to make an appointment for a while now, but I just kept putting it off. But it’s gotten to the point where I just feel like I cannot settle inside my own skin anymore. It’s the most awful, frustrating feeling. \n\nMy husband bought me this lovely plush recliner a while back when I stopped working, so I could finally just rest, you know? But I can't even use it. I'll sit down with a magazine or something, and I swear, not even two minutes go by and I just get this... this crawling feeling, like I absolutely have to jump up and do the next thing. And the silliest part of it all is that I am completely worn out. I'm 66 years old, I'm exhausted, and I really should be sitting still and just enjoying a cup of tea or a television program. But I just can't do it. I'm up reorganizing the linen closet for the third time this month or pacing around the kitchen wiping down counters that are already clean, and I just don't know how to turn it off. It's like my body is forcing me to keep moving even though I'm so tired I could cry.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to get up and do these things, is it because of a specific fear or thought that something bad will happen if you don’t?\n\nPatient: No, no, it’s nothing like that. I mean, I don't sit there thinking the house is going to burn down or that someone is going to get hurt if I don't fold the towels right that second. It’s not a worry in my head, if that makes sense. It's more like... well, it’s just this physical urge. Like an itch you can't reach, but it's on the inside. \n\nI remember last Tuesday, my husband, Jim—he's such a patient man, bless him—he had made us a nice pot of Earl Grey, and he said, \"Martha, just sit with me for a bit. The news is on.\" And I really wanted to. I had my cup, I had a little biscuit, and I sat in that recliner. But almost immediately, my legs started to feel twitchy. Not painful, just... restless. And then my mind just started zooming to the fact that the dog's toys were scattered in the hallway, and the mail from yesterday was still on the entryway table, and before I knew it, I had left my tea on the coaster—which I had to straighten three times, mind you—and I was up gathering the toys. Jim just sighed. He didn't say anything, but I could tell he was disappointed. And I was disappointed in myself! I just wanted to drink my tea. But there’s no voice telling me something terrible will happen, it’s just this motor inside me that won't shut off, even when my bones are aching and I just want to rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different things most days, even when there isn’t a specific reason to worry?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm a worrier. I mean, everyone worries a little bit, don't they? Like when Jim's cholesterol was high last year, of course I worried about that, and I made sure we switched to leaner meats and cut back on the butter, which was hard because Jim loves his butter on a baked potato. But no, I don't just sit around agonizing over things for no reason. It's not my mind that's troubled like that, it's just my body that won't stay put. \n\nMy sister, Elaine, now she's a worrier. She'll call me up on a Tuesday morning and talk for an hour about how she's sure the neighborhood stray cat is going to freeze, or how she thinks her grandson isn't eating enough vegetables, and she just works herself up into a real state over it. I always tell her, \"Elaine, you can't control the whole world,\" but she just can't help it. I'm not like that at all. My mind is busy, yes, but it's busy thinking about the fifty things I need to do around the house, not worrying that the sky is falling. Like yesterday, I wasn't worried about the laundry, I just couldn't stop thinking about the fact that the towels in the guest bathroom needed to be washed, dried, folded, and put back before I could even think about sitting down. And then once that was done, I noticed the baseboards... but it's never out of fear or anxiety, just this endless to-do list that my brain won't let me ignore.\n\nDoctor: Have you started or changed any medications, supplements, or other substances around the time these symptoms began?\n\nPatient: Medications? Oh, goodness, let me think. No, I haven't changed a single thing. I mean, I take a little calcium for my bones, you know, just what Dr. Henderson—my regular doctor—told me to take years ago. He always says, \"Martha, at your age, we just need to keep the bones strong,\" so I take that, and a standard daily multivitamin, but I've been taking those exact same ones since I turned sixty. I buy the big bottles at Costco so I don't have to keep going back every month. \n\nJim, now, he has a whole plastic pillbox because of the cholesterol I mentioned, and his blood pressure, and I'm the one who organizes all of that for him every Sunday evening right after dinner. I have this little system with the days of the week, because otherwise he'd just forget entirely and leave them sitting right there on the kitchen island. But for me? No, nothing new. \n\nMy sister Elaine is always trying to get me to take these herbal things she reads about on the internet—she sent me a whole care package of some root powder last fall that she swore would give me more energy, which is just a laugh because energy is the absolute last thing I need right now! I told her, \"Elaine, I'm practically vibrating out of my chair most days, why on earth would I take an energy pill?\" So I just put that whole bottle straight in the trash. I don't like messing with things like that anyway, you never know how it's going to react with your stomach or if it's even safe. So, to answer your question, no, no new medications or supplements or anything at all. It's just my same old routine, which is why it's so baffling to me that I can't just sit still. It's not coming from a pill, it's just... me.\n\nDoctor: When you feel driven to clean, straighten, or reorganize things, do you also get unwanted intrusive thoughts or images that keep coming back and make you feel you have to do it?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that at all. Intrusive thoughts... no, I don't get anything like that. It's not like I'm seeing pictures in my head of terrible things happening, or having these awful thoughts that I just can't shake. It's really just the opposite, honestly. My mind is just so... practical, I suppose. \n\nLike I was saying about the towels, I just see that they need to be done, and then I have to do them. It's the same with the kitchen counters. After Jim makes his sandwich—he loves a turkey and Swiss on rye for lunch, every single day at noon—he always leaves a few crumbs on the counter. He tries to wipe them up, he really does, but he uses a dry paper towel and just sort of pushes them around. So I see the crumbs, and I think, \"Well, I need to wipe those up properly.\" And then I get the sponge, and I wipe the crumbs, but then I see a little spot near the toaster, so I clean that, and then the toaster looks a bit smudged, so I get the stainless steel cleaner from under the sink... and before I know it, I've spent forty-five minutes scrubbing the entire kitchen, pulling the appliances out, wiping down the cabinet doors. \n\nBut it's never because I had a bad thought! I'm not thinking, \"Oh, if I don't clean this crumb, the whole house will be infested with bugs and we'll have to move.\" I don't think like that. It's just that I start a task, and then I see the next thing, and the next thing, and my body just won't let me stop until I'm absolutely exhausted. I just can't seem to put the brakes on, you know? But no, my mind isn't playing tricks on me with scary pictures or unwanted thoughts. It's just... I see a mess, and I have to fix it, right then and there.\n\nDoctor: At what age did these restlessness and compulsive cleaning symptoms first begin?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, what age? Well, if I really sit down and think about it—and Jim and I have actually talked about this, because he says I've been an absolute whirlwind since the day we met at the church social back in '76—it's not like I just woke up one day recently and started running around with a sponge. The truth is, it’s always been there. It's just changed shape, I suppose.\n\nI mean, even way back in grade school... I remember my mother kept all my old report cards in this heavy cedar chest at the foot of her bed, and if you looked at them, every single teacher wrote the exact same thing. They'd say that I was a bright girl, but just so careless. I was always losing my spelling papers, or leaving my arithmetic homework sitting right on the kitchen table after my mother had sat there and made sure I did it. And the talking! Oh, I was always talking out of turn, just blurting things out before the teacher even finished her sentence. \n\nMy third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—she was a lovely woman but very strict, wore these thick horn-rimmed glasses and always smelled like peppermint—she was constantly having to remind me to just stay in my seat. I just couldn't stay put, even then. I'd be up sharpening my pencil for the fifth time, or looking out the window, or trying to talk to the boy next to me. And finishing my work? Well, she was always having to stand over me to make sure I actually finished a worksheet, because I'd get halfway through and then I was off on something else entirely. \n\nSo, you see, the restlessness isn't new at all. It’s just that back then, I was supposed to be sitting quietly at a little wooden desk, and now I have a whole house to manage. The cleaning is just what's in front of me to do now, but that feeling of a motor just running and running inside me, making me get up and move and bounce from one task to the next... that's been there since I was a little girl in pigtails. I just never really stopped to think about it as a problem until lately, because I'm getting older and I just wear myself out so completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble sitting still in other situations, like at church, in meetings, or while watching a movie?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, sitting still... well, Jim actually gave up trying to take me to the picture show years ago. We used to go to the matinees over at the Bijou on Tuesdays because they have that nice senior discount, but I’d be up and down to the concession stand, or the restroom, or just wiggling in those velvet seats until poor Jim couldn't even hear the dialogue. So we just watch television at home now, where I can at least fold laundry or dust the coffee table while the screen is on. \n\nBut you mentioned church, and oh, that is a whole other ordeal. We go to First Methodist, have been for forty years, and I am very involved with the Women's Auxiliary. We have our planning meetings every Wednesday morning in the fellowship hall. And even there, where I'm supposed to be paying attention to Pastor Dave or listening to the treasurer's report, I just cannot for the life of me sit quietly. It's honestly embarrassing. \n\nI'll be sitting there in those metal folding chairs—which are terribly uncomfortable to begin with, but I suppose that's beside the point—and I'll realize I'm just tapping my foot, tap-tap-tapping away on the linoleum. And if I force my foot to stop, then my hands start going. I carry this nice leather handbag, and I'll find myself just rubbing and rubbing the edge of my purse, over and over, just tracing the seam with my thumb. And then my back gets restless, so I'm shifting around in the chair, crossing my legs, uncrossing them, leaning this way and that, trying to get comfortable but never actually settling. \n\nIt gets to the point where the ladies beside me—usually it's Susan Miller and Betty Higgins—they'll actually stop paying attention to the meeting and start looking over at me. Susan will give me this little sideways glance over her reading glasses, like she's wondering if I've had three pots of coffee for breakfast. I always try to just smile and pretend I'm just adjusting my skirt, but the truth is, I just feel like I'm going to jump right out of my skin if I have to sit frozen in one spot. It takes so much effort just to try and look like a normal person sitting in a chair, you know?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I see a mess, and I have to fix it, right then and there.\n\nDoctor: At what age did these restlessness and compulsive cleaning symptoms first begin?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, what age? Well, if I really sit down and think about it—and Jim and I have actually talked about this, because he says I've been an absolute whirlwind since the day we met at the church social back in '76—it's not like I just woke up one day recently and started running around with a sponge. The truth is, it’s always been there. It's just changed shape, I suppose.\n\nI mean, even way back in grade school... I remember my mother kept all my old report cards in this heavy cedar chest at the foot of her bed, and if you looked at them, every single teacher wrote the exact same thing. They'd say that I was a bright girl, but just so careless. I was always losing my spelling papers, or leaving my arithmetic homework sitting right on the kitchen table after my mother had sat there and made sure I did it. And the talking! Oh, I was always talking out of turn, just blurting things out before the teacher even finished her sentence. \n\nMy third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—she was a lovely woman but very strict, wore these thick horn-rimmed glasses and always smelled like peppermint—she was constantly having to remind me to just stay in my seat. I just couldn't stay put, even then. I'd be up sharpening my pencil for the fifth time, or looking out the window, or trying to talk to the boy next to me. And finishing my work? Well, she was always having to stand over me to make sure I actually finished a worksheet, because I'd get halfway through and then I was off on something else entirely. \n\nSo, you see, the restlessness isn't new at all. It’s just that back then, I was supposed to be sitting quietly at a little wooden desk, and now I have a whole house to manage. The cleaning is just what's in front of me to do now, but that feeling of a motor just running and running inside me, making me get up and move and bounce from one task to the next... that's been there since I was a little girl in pigtails. I just never really stopped to think about it as a problem until lately, because I'm getting older and I just wear myself out so completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble sitting still in other situations, like at church, in meetings, or while watching a movie?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, sitting still... well, Jim actually gave up trying to take me to the picture show years ago. We used to go to the matinees over at the Bijou on Tuesdays because they have that nice senior discount, but I’d be up and down to the concession stand, or the restroom, or just wiggling in those velvet seats until poor Jim couldn't even hear the dialogue. So we just watch television at home now, where I can at least fold laundry or dust the coffee table while the screen is on. \n\nBut you mentioned church, and oh, that is a whole other ordeal. We go to First Methodist, have been for forty years, and I am very involved with the Women's Auxiliary. We have our planning meetings every Wednesday morning in the fellowship hall. And even there, where I'm supposed to be paying attention to Pastor Dave or listening to the treasurer's report, I just cannot for the life of me sit quietly. It's honestly embarrassing. \n\nI'll be sitting there in those metal folding chairs—which are terribly uncomfortable to begin with, but I suppose that's beside the point—and I'll realize I'm just tapping my foot, tap-tap-tapping away on the linoleum. And if I force my foot to stop, then my hands start going. I carry this nice leather handbag, and I'll find myself just rubbing and rubbing the edge of my purse, over and over, just tracing the seam with my thumb. And then my back gets restless, so I'm shifting around in the chair, crossing my legs, uncrossing them, leaning this way and that, trying to get comfortable but never actually settling. \n\nIt gets to the point where the ladies beside me—usually it's Susan Miller and Betty Higgins—they'll actually stop paying attention to the meeting and start looking over at me. Susan will give me this little sideways glance over her reading glasses, like she's wondering if I've had three pots of coffee for breakfast. I always try to just smile and pretend I'm just adjusting my skirt, but the truth is, I just feel like I'm going to jump right out of my skin if I have to sit frozen in one spot. It takes so much effort just to try and look like a normal person sitting in a chair, you know?\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period of at least several days when you felt unusually high, overly energized, or euphoric compared with your usual self?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Unusually high? Or euphoric? Oh, heavens no. I mean, I know I just sat here and told you how I'm always moving and how I have this motor running inside me that just won't quit, but that’s not... it’s not a *high* feeling. It’s certainly not euphoric. If anything, it’s just exhausting! 

Jim sometimes jokes that I have more energy than his old hunting dog, Buster—God rest his soul, Buster was a beautiful golden retriever we had back in the eighties, just the sweetest thing, but he would run circles around the oak tree in the backyard until he literally made a dirt rut in the grass. But even Buster would eventually sleep on the porch. With me, it's not like I wake up one Tuesday and suddenly feel like I'm on top of the world and can do a million grand things for a few days, and then it goes away. No, no. It's just my normal, everyday state of being. 

And it's not a happy, euphoric kind of energy, either. It's more like a... a pressure, I suppose? Like I was saying before, I just *have* to do these things, like I have to wipe the counter or fold the towels or tap my foot. I don't feel grand or overly joyful about it. I mean, of course I get happy, like normal people do! When our oldest grandson, Michael, graduated from college last spring—he went to State, got a degree in business, we were just so incredibly proud of him because he worked so hard—I was absolutely thrilled, just over the moon! We had a big party in the backyard, ordered a beautiful sheet cake from that little bakery down on Elm Street, the one that does the lovely buttercream roses, and they put a little diploma on it. But that was just being a proud grandmother, you know? It wasn't some strange, unusual high where I wasn't myself. 

So, no, I've never had a time where I felt like that, like I was euphoric or abnormally energized for days on end. It's just the same old me, every single day, just buzzing along, trying to keep up with myself. I honestly don't even know what it would feel like to be euphoric, unless it means finally being able to sit still through a whole episode of Jeopardy without getting up to dust the television stand!

I'll sit down with a magazine or something, and I swear, not even two minutes go by and I just get this... this crawling feeling, like I absolutely have to jump up and do the next thing. And the silliest part of it all is that I am completely worn out. I'm 66 years old, I'm exhausted, and I really should be sitting still and just enjoying a cup of tea or a television program. But I just can't do it. Like an itch you can't reach, but it's on the inside. \n\nI remember last Tuesday, my husband, Jim—he's such a patient man, bless him—he had made us a nice pot of Earl Grey, and he said, \"Martha, just sit with me for a bit. The news is on.\" And I really wanted to. I had my cup, I had a little biscuit, and I sat in that recliner. But almost immediately, my legs started to feel twitchy. Not painful, just... restless. Jim just sighed. He didn't say anything, but I could tell he was disappointed. And I was disappointed in myself! I just wanted to drink my tea. But there’s no voice telling me something terrible will happen, it’s just this motor inside me that won't shut off, even when my bones are aching and I just want to rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different things most days, even when there isn’t a specific reason to worry?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm a worrier. I don't like messing with things like that anyway, you never know how it's going to react with your stomach or if it's even safe. I mean, of course I get happy, like normal people do! When our oldest grandson, Michael, graduated from college last spring—he went to State, got a degree in business, we were just so incredibly proud of him because he worked so hard—I was absolutely thrilled, just over the moon! We had a big party in the backyard, ordered a beautiful sheet cake from that little bakery down on Elm Street, the one that does the lovely buttercream roses, and they put a little diploma on it. But that was just being a proud grandmother, you know? It wasn't some strange, unusual high where I wasn't myself. \n\nSo, no, I've never had a time where I felt like that, like I was euphoric or abnormally energized for days on end. It's just the same old me, every single day, just buzzing along, trying to keep up with myself. I honestly don't even know what it would feel like to be euphoric, unless it means finally being able to sit still through a whole episode of Jeopardy without getting up to dust the television stand!\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don't like messing with things like that anyway, you never know how it's going to react with your stomach or if it's even safe. It's really just the opposite, honestly. My mind is just so... practical, I suppose. \n\nLike I was saying about the towels, I just see that they need to be done, and then I have to do them. I'm not thinking, \"Oh, if I don't clean this crumb, the whole house will be infested with bugs and we'll have to move.\" I don't think like that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, heavens, yes. Like I was telling you before about leaving my arithmetic right there on the kitchen table, or losing my spelling papers... I was just a disaster with the details back then. I'd forget to carry the one in math, or I'd skip a whole question on a test just because I was looking at a robin out the window and completely lost my place. 

But the terrible truth of it is, Doctor, I never really outgrew it. It’s actually one of the things that frustrates Jim the most, and it frustrates me, too, to be perfectly honest. I keep making these little mistakes that just snowball and turn into absolute big messes. 

Take the garden club, for instance. I somehow let them talk me into being the treasurer this year—which was my first mistake, really, knowing how I am with numbers. Just last Tuesday, it was pouring rain, and I was trying to balance the accounts and write a check for the new spring mulch we ordered from Miller’s Nursery. Well, the phone rang, and it was my sister Margaret going on about her sciatica, and by the time I hung up, I was completely distracted. I ended up writing the completely wrong amount in the garden checkbook! I wrote eighty-five dollars instead of fifty-eight, just flipped the numbers right around in my head, and it threw the whole ledger out of balance. I spent three hours crying at the kitchen table trying to figure out where the missing money went before Jim finally sat down and found the error. 

And it’s not just numbers, either. It’s reading things, too. Just last month, we had our big autumn church supper. I was in charge of the master sign-up list, and I sat there in my favorite armchair with my good reading glasses on, and I swear to you, I thought I checked that list three times. I really, truly believed I had gone down every single line. But somehow, I managed to leave Betty Higgins and her husband Tom completely off the list! They showed up to the fellowship hall carrying this beautiful green bean casserole—the one with the crispy fried onions on top, Betty makes it every year—and they didn't have a table assignment. I was absolutely mortified. I felt about two inches tall. 

So, yes, the careless mistakes from grade school... they never really stopped. They just changed from spelling tests into checkbooks and church suppers, no matter how hard I try to pay attention.

Well, I suppose I'm here because... well, I've been meaning to make an appointment for a while now, but I just kept putting it off. But it’s gotten to the point where I just feel like I cannot settle inside my own skin anymore. It’s the most awful, frustrating feeling. \n\nMy husband bought me this lovely plush recliner a while back when I stopped working, so I could finally just rest, you know? But I can't even use it. I'll sit down with a magazine or something, and I swear, not even two minutes go by and I just get this... this crawling feeling, like I absolutely have to jump up and do the next thing. And the silliest part of it all is that I am completely worn out. I'm 66 years old, I'm exhausted, and I really should be sitting still and just enjoying a cup of tea or a television program. But I just can't do it. I'm up reorganizing the linen closet for the third time this month or pacing around the kitchen wiping down counters that are already clean, and I just don't know how to turn it off. It's like my body is forcing me to keep moving even though I'm so tired I could cry.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to get up and do these things, is it because of a specific fear or thought that something bad will happen if you don’t?\n\nPatient: No, no, it’s nothing like that. I mean, I don't sit there thinking the house is going to burn down or that someone is going to get hurt if I don't fold the towels right that second. It’s not a worry in my head, if that makes sense. It's more like... well, it’s just this physical urge. Like an itch you can't reach, but it's on the inside. \n\nI remember last Tuesday, my husband, Jim—he's such a patient man, bless him—he had made us a nice pot of Earl Grey, and he said, \"Martha, just sit with me for a bit. The news is on.\" And I really wanted to. I had my cup, I had a little biscuit, and I sat in that recliner. But almost immediately, my legs started to feel twitchy. Not painful, just... restless. And then my mind just started zooming to the fact that the dog's toys were scattered in the hallway, and the mail from yesterday was still on the entryway table, and before I knew it, I had left my tea on the coaster—which I had to straighten three times, mind you—and I was up gathering the toys. Jim just sighed. He didn't say anything, but I could tell he was disappointed. And I was disappointed in myself! I just wanted to drink my tea. But there’s no voice telling me something terrible will happen, it’s just this motor inside me that won't shut off, even when my bones are aching and I just want to rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different things most days, even when there isn’t a specific reason to worry?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm a worrier. I mean, everyone worries a little bit, don't they? Like when Jim's cholesterol was high last year, of course I worried about that, and I made sure we switched to leaner meats and cut back on the butter, which was hard because Jim loves his butter on a baked potato. But no, I don't just sit around agonizing over things for no reason. It's not my mind that's troubled like that, it's just my body that won't stay put. \n\nMy sister, Elaine, now she's a worrier. She'll call me up on a Tuesday morning and talk for an hour about how she's sure the neighborhood stray cat is going to freeze, or how she thinks her grandson isn't eating enough vegetables, and she just works herself up into a real state over it. I always tell her, \"Elaine, you can't control the whole world,\" but she just can't help it. I'm not like that at all. My mind is busy, yes, but it's busy thinking about the fifty things I need to do around the house, not worrying that the sky is falling. Like yesterday, I wasn't worried about the laundry, I just couldn't stop thinking about the fact that the towels in the guest bathroom needed to be washed, dried, folded, and put back before I could even think about sitting down. And then once that was done, I noticed the baseboards... but it's never out of fear or anxiety, just this endless to-do list that my brain won't let me ignore.\n\nDoctor: Have you started or changed any medications, supplements, or other substances around the time these symptoms began?\n\nPatient: Medications? Oh, goodness, let me think. No, I haven't changed a single thing. I mean, I take a little calcium for my bones, you know, just what Dr. Henderson—my regular doctor—told me to take years ago. He always says, \"Martha, at your age, we just need to keep the bones strong,\" so I take that, and a standard daily multivitamin, but I've been taking those exact same ones since I turned sixty. I buy the big bottles at Costco so I don't have to keep going back every month. \n\nJim, now, he has a whole plastic pillbox because of the cholesterol I mentioned, and his blood pressure, and I'm the one who organizes all of that for him every Sunday evening right after dinner. I have this little system with the days of the week, because otherwise he'd just forget entirely and leave them sitting right there on the kitchen island. But for me? No, nothing new. \n\nMy sister Elaine is always trying to get me to take these herbal things she reads about on the internet—she sent me a whole care package of some root powder last fall that she swore would give me more energy, which is just a laugh because energy is the absolute last thing I need right now! I told her, \"Elaine, I'm practically vibrating out of my chair most days, why on earth would I take an energy pill?\" So I just put that whole bottle straight in the trash. I don't like messing with things like that anyway, you never know how it's going to react with your stomach or if it's even safe. So, to answer your question, no, no new medications or supplements or anything at all. It's just my same old routine, which is why it's so baffling to me that I can't just sit still. It's not coming from a pill, it's just... me.\n\nDoctor: When you feel driven to clean, straighten, or reorganize things, do you also get unwanted intrusive thoughts or images that keep coming back and make you feel you have to do it?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that at all. Intrusive thoughts... no, I don't get anything like that. It's not like I'm seeing pictures in my head of terrible things happening, or having these awful thoughts that I just can't shake. It's really just the opposite, honestly. My mind is just so... practical, I suppose. \n\nLike I was saying about the towels, I just see that they need to be done, and then I have to do them. It's the same with the kitchen counters. After Jim makes his sandwich—he loves a turkey and Swiss on rye for lunch, every single day at noon—he always leaves a few crumbs on the counter. He tries to wipe them up, he really does, but he uses a dry paper towel and just sort of pushes them around. So I see the crumbs, and I think, \"Well, I need to wipe those up properly.\" And then I get the sponge, and I wipe the crumbs, but then I see a little spot near the toaster, so I clean that, and then the toaster looks a bit smudged, so I get the stainless steel cleaner from under the sink... and before I know it, I've spent forty-five minutes scrubbing the entire kitchen, pulling the appliances out, wiping down the cabinet doors. \n\nBut it's never because I had a bad thought! I'm not thinking, \"Oh, if I don't clean this crumb, the whole house will be infested with bugs and we'll have to move.\" I don't think like that. It's just that I start a task, and then I see the next thing, and the next thing, and my body just won't let me stop until I'm absolutely exhausted. I just can't seem to put the brakes on, you know? But no, my mind isn't playing tricks on me with scary pictures or unwanted thoughts. It's just... I see a mess, and I have to fix it, right then and there.\n\nDoctor: At what age did these restlessness and compulsive cleaning symptoms first begin?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, what age? Well, if I really sit down and think about it—and Jim and I have actually talked about this, because he says I've been an absolute whirlwind since the day we met at the church social back in '76—it's not like I just woke up one day recently and started running around with a sponge. The truth is, it’s always been there. It's just changed shape, I suppose.\n\nI mean, even way back in grade school... I remember my mother kept all my old report cards in this heavy cedar chest at the foot of her bed, and if you looked at them, every single teacher wrote the exact same thing. They'd say that I was a bright girl, but just so careless. I was always losing my spelling papers, or leaving my arithmetic homework sitting right on the kitchen table after my mother had sat there and made sure I did it. And the talking! Oh, I was always talking out of turn, just blurting things out before the teacher even finished her sentence. \n\nMy third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—she was a lovely woman but very strict, wore these thick horn-rimmed glasses and always smelled like peppermint—she was constantly having to remind me to just stay in my seat. I just couldn't stay put, even then. I'd be up sharpening my pencil for the fifth time, or looking out the window, or trying to talk to the boy next to me. And finishing my work? Well, she was always having to stand over me to make sure I actually finished a worksheet, because I'd get halfway through and then I was off on something else entirely. \n\nSo, you see, the restlessness isn't new at all. It’s just that back then, I was supposed to be sitting quietly at a little wooden desk, and now I have a whole house to manage. The cleaning is just what's in front of me to do now, but that feeling of a motor just running and running inside me, making me get up and move and bounce from one task to the next... that's been there since I was a little girl in pigtails. I just never really stopped to think about it as a problem until lately, because I'm getting older and I just wear myself out so completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble sitting still in other situations, like at church, in meetings, or while watching a movie?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, sitting still... well, Jim actually gave up trying to take me to the picture show years ago. We used to go to the matinees over at the Bijou on Tuesdays because they have that nice senior discount, but I’d be up and down to the concession stand, or the restroom, or just wiggling in those velvet seats until poor Jim couldn't even hear the dialogue. So we just watch television at home now, where I can at least fold laundry or dust the coffee table while the screen is on. \n\nBut you mentioned church, and oh, that is a whole other ordeal. We go to First Methodist, have been for forty years, and I am very involved with the Women's Auxiliary. We have our planning meetings every Wednesday morning in the fellowship hall. And even there, where I'm supposed to be paying attention to Pastor Dave or listening to the treasurer's report, I just cannot for the life of me sit quietly. It's honestly embarrassing. \n\nI'll be sitting there in those metal folding chairs—which are terribly uncomfortable to begin with, but I suppose that's beside the point—and I'll realize I'm just tapping my foot, tap-tap-tapping away on the linoleum. And if I force my foot to stop, then my hands start going. I carry this nice leather handbag, and I'll find myself just rubbing and rubbing the edge of my purse, over and over, just tracing the seam with my thumb. And then my back gets restless, so I'm shifting around in the chair, crossing my legs, uncrossing them, leaning this way and that, trying to get comfortable but never actually settling. \n\nIt gets to the point where the ladies beside me—usually it's Susan Miller and Betty Higgins—they'll actually stop paying attention to the meeting and start looking over at me. Susan will give me this little sideways glance over her reading glasses, like she's wondering if I've had three pots of coffee for breakfast. I always try to just smile and pretend I'm just adjusting my skirt, but the truth is, I just feel like I'm going to jump right out of my skin if I have to sit frozen in one spot. It takes so much effort just to try and look like a normal person sitting in a chair, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least several days when you felt unusually high, overly energized, or euphoric compared with your usual self?\n\nPatient: Unusually high? Or euphoric? Oh, heavens no. I mean, I know I just sat here and told you how I'm always moving and how I have this motor running inside me that just won't quit, but that’s not... it’s not a *high* feeling. It’s certainly not euphoric. If anything, it’s just exhausting! \n\nJim sometimes jokes that I have more energy than his old hunting dog, Buster—God rest his soul, Buster was a beautiful golden retriever we had back in the eighties, just the sweetest thing, but he would run circles around the oak tree in the backyard until he literally made a dirt rut in the grass. But even Buster would eventually sleep on the porch. With me, it's not like I wake up one Tuesday and suddenly feel like I'm on top of the world and can do a million grand things for a few days, and then it goes away. No, no. It's just my normal, everyday state of being. \n\nAnd it's not a happy, euphoric kind of energy, either. It's more like a... a pressure, I suppose? Like I was saying before, I just *have* to do these things, like I have to wipe the counter or fold the towels or tap my foot. I don't feel grand or overly joyful about it. I mean, of course I get happy, like normal people do! When our oldest grandson, Michael, graduated from college last spring—he went to State, got a degree in business, we were just so incredibly proud of him because he worked so hard—I was absolutely thrilled, just over the moon! We had a big party in the backyard, ordered a beautiful sheet cake from that little bakery down on Elm Street, the one that does the lovely buttercream roses, and they put a little diploma on it. But that was just being a proud grandmother, you know? It wasn't some strange, unusual high where I wasn't myself. \n\nSo, no, I've never had a time where I felt like that, like I was euphoric or abnormally energized for days on end. It's just the same old me, every single day, just buzzing along, trying to keep up with myself. I honestly don't even know what it would feel like to be euphoric, unless it means finally being able to sit still through a whole episode of Jeopardy without getting up to dust the television stand!\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you also often make careless mistakes or have trouble finishing homework because you got distracted?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. Like I was telling you before about leaving my arithmetic right there on the kitchen table, or losing my spelling papers... I was just a disaster with the details back then. I'd forget to carry the one in math, or I'd skip a whole question on a test just because I was looking at a robin out the window and completely lost my place. \n\nBut the terrible truth of it is, Doctor, I never really outgrew it. I keep making these little mistakes that just snowball and turn into absolute big messes. \n\nTake the garden club, for instance. I somehow let them talk me into being the treasurer this year—which was my first mistake, really, knowing how I am with numbers. Just last Tuesday, it was pouring rain, and I was trying to balance the accounts and write a check for the new spring mulch we ordered from Miller’s Nursery. Well, the phone rang, and it was my sister Margaret going on about her sciatica, and by the time I hung up, I was completely distracted. I ended up writing the completely wrong amount in the garden checkbook! I wrote eighty-five dollars instead of fifty-eight, just flipped the numbers right around in my head, and it threw the whole ledger out of balance. I spent three hours crying at the kitchen table trying to figure out where the missing money went before Jim finally sat down and found the error. \n\nAnd it’s not just numbers, either. It’s reading things, too. Just last month, we had our big autumn church supper. I was in charge of the master sign-up list, and I sat there in my favorite armchair with my good reading glasses on, and I swear to you, I thought I checked that list three times. I really, truly believed I had gone down every single line. But somehow, I managed to leave Betty Higgins and her husband Tom completely off the list! They showed up to the fellowship hall carrying this beautiful green bean casserole—the one with the crispy fried onions on top, Betty makes it every year—and they didn't have a table assignment. I was absolutely mortified. I felt about two inches tall. \n\nSo, yes, the careless mistakes from grade school... they never really stopped. They just changed from spelling tests into checkbooks and church suppers, no matter how hard I try to pay attention.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, goodness, no. Symmetrical? Just right? Jim would probably laugh himself silly if he heard you ask that. My sister Margaret, now she is exactly like that. She has her decorative throw pillows lined up so perfectly on her davenport you’d think she used a tape measure, and if you even look like you're going to sit on one, she practically fluffs it before you've even stood all the way back up! But me? Heavens, no. 

When I'm cleaning or arranging things—like when I’m dusting the coffee table or folding the laundry while the television is on, like I mentioned earlier—it's never because I need the towels folded in perfect little identical squares. It's really just because my hands need something to do. I don't have that... that urge to make everything look perfect. Half the time, to be perfectly honest, I don't even finish arranging a space once I start! I'll be trying to organize the magazines on the end table—we get Reader's Digest and Southern Living, and Jim likes his Field & Stream—and I'll stack maybe three of them, but then I'll spot a smudge on the lamp base, so I'll drop the magazines all crooked to go get the glass cleaner, and then I usually never get back to the magazines at all. 

So I certainly don't have this feeling that things need to be perfectly lined up or... what was the word, symmetrical? No, not at all. If I can just get the living room looking halfway presentable before Jim's brother comes over for Sunday dinner, I consider that a grand victory. I just don't have the patience to make anything look 'just right.' I'm buzzing around entirely too fast to worry about whether the brass candlesticks on the mantle are exactly two inches from the edge. It's just about keeping moving for me, not about making it perfect.
